|  ![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/96/Symbol_category_class.svg/20px-Symbol_category_class.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)[Category: Titanium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Titanium "Category:Titanium")

|[references](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_data_references_for_chemical_elements "List of data references for chemical elements")  |  
**Titanium** is a [chemical element](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_element "Chemical element"); it has [symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbol_\(chemistry\) "Symbol \(chemistry\)") **Ti** and [atomic number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_number "Atomic number") 22. Found in nature only as an [oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxide "Oxide"), it can be reduced to produce a lustrous [transition metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transition_metal "Transition metal") with a silver [color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Color "Color"), low [density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Density "Density"), and high strength that is resistant to [corrosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrosion "Corrosion") in [sea water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_water "Sea water"), [aqua regia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqua_regia "Aqua regia"), and [chlorine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chlorine "Chlorine").
Titanium was discovered in [Cornwall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall "Cornwall"), [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain "Kingdom of Great Britain"), by [William Gregor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Gregor "William Gregor") in 1791 and was named by [Martin Heinrich Klaproth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Heinrich_Klaproth "Martin Heinrich Klaproth") after the [Titans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_\(mythology\) "Titan \(mythology\)") of [Greek mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology "Greek mythology"). The element occurs within a number of [minerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral "Mineral"), principally [rutile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutile "Rutile") and [ilmenite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilmenite "Ilmenite"), which are widely distributed in the [Earth's crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_crust "Earth's crust") and [lithosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithosphere "Lithosphere"); it is found in almost all living things, as well as bodies of water, rocks, and soils.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EBC-13) The metal is extracted from its principal mineral ores by the [Kroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kroll_process "Kroll process") and [Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_process "Hunter process") processes.[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14) The most common compound, [titanium dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_dioxide "Titanium dioxide") (TiO2), is a popular [photocatalyst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photocatalysis "Photocatalysis") and is used in the manufacture of white pigments.[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-HistoryAndUse-15) Other compounds include [titanium tetrachloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_tetrachloride "Titanium tetrachloride") (TiCl4), a component of [smoke screens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoke_screen "Smoke screen") and [catalysts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalyst "Catalyst"); and [titanium trichloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_trichloride "Titanium trichloride") (TiCl3), which is used as a catalyst in the production of [polypropylene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polypropylene "Polypropylene").[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EBC-13)
Titanium can be [alloyed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alloy "Alloy") with [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron "Iron"), [aluminium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aluminium "Aluminium"), [vanadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanadium "Vanadium"), and [molybdenum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molybdenum "Molybdenum"), among other elements. The resulting [titanium alloys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_alloys "Titanium alloys") are strong, lightweight, and versatile, with applications including aerospace ([jet engines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_engine "Jet engine"), [missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missile "Missile"), and [spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spacecraft "Spacecraft")), military, industrial processes (chemicals and petrochemicals, [desalination plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desalination_plant "Desalination plant"), pulp, and paper), automotive, [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture "Agriculture") (farming), sporting goods, jewelry, and [consumer electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_electronics "Consumer electronics").[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EBC-13) Titanium is also considered one of the most [biocompatible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biocompatible "Biocompatible") metals, leading to a range of medical applications including [prostheses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostheses "Prostheses"), [orthopedic implants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthopedic_implant "Orthopedic implant"), [dental implants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dental_implant "Dental implant"), and [surgical instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surgical_instrument "Surgical instrument").[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Medical_University_2022_v636-16)
The two most useful properties of the metal are its corrosion resistance and [tensile-strength-to-density ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Specific_strength "Specific strength"), the highest of any metallic element,[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-17) although resistance to [tensile stress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tensile_stress "Tensile stress") does not mean it has the highest ratio for other stresses: [bulk compression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulk_modulus "Bulk modulus"), [shear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shear_stress "Shear stress"), and [pressure wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P-wave_modulus "P-wave modulus"). In its unalloyed condition, titanium is as tensile-strong as some [steels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel "Steel"), but less dense.[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p738-18) There are two [allotropic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allotropy "Allotropy") forms[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-TICE6th-19) and five naturally occurring [isotopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotope "Isotope") of this element, [46Ti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_titanium "Isotopes of titanium") through 50Ti, with 48Ti being the most [abundant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_abundance "Natural abundance") (73.8%).[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EnvChem-20)
## Characteristics
### Physical properties
[strength-to-weight ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strength-to-weight_ratio "Strength-to-weight ratio").[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-TICE6th-19) It is a strong metal with low [density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Density "Density") that is quite [ductile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ductility "Ductility") (especially in an [oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen "Oxygen")-free environment),[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EBC-13) lustrous, and metallic-white in color.[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Stwertka1998-21) Due to its relatively high melting point (1,668°C or 3,034°F) it has sometimes been described as a [refractory metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refractory_metals "Refractory metals"), but this is not the case.[[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-22) It is [paramagnetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramagnetism "Paramagnetism") and has fairly low [electrical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_conductivity "Electrical conductivity") and [thermal conductivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_conductivity "Thermal conductivity") compared to other metals.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EBC-13) Titanium is [superconducting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superconductivity "Superconductivity") when cooled below its critical temperature of 0.49K.[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-23)[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-24)
Commercially pure (99.2% pure) [grades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_alloy#Grades_of_titanium "Titanium alloy") of titanium have [ultimate tensile strength](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultimate_tensile_strength "Ultimate tensile strength") of about 434[MPa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megapascal "Megapascal") (63,000[psi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pounds_per_square_inch "Pounds per square inch")), equal to that of common, low-grade steel alloys, but are less dense. Titanium is 60% denser than [aluminium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aluminium "Aluminium"), but more than twice as strong[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p738-18) as the most commonly used [6061-T6 aluminium alloy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6061_aluminium_alloy "6061 aluminium alloy"). Certain titanium alloys (e.g., [Beta C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_Beta_C "Titanium Beta C")) achieve tensile strengths of over 1,400MPa (200,000psi).[[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-25) However, titanium loses strength when heated above 430°C (806°F).[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p734-26)
Titanium is not as hard as some grades of heat-treated steel; it is non-magnetic and a poor conductor of [heat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_conduction "Thermal conduction") and [electricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_conductor "Electrical conductor"). Machining requires precautions, because the material can [gall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galling "Galling") unless sharp tools and proper cooling methods are used. Like steel structures, those made from titanium have a [fatigue limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatigue_limit "Fatigue limit") that guarantees longevity in some applications.[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Stwertka1998-21)
The metal is a dimorphic [allotrope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allotrope "Allotrope") of a [hexagonal close packed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hexagonal_close_packed "Hexagonal close packed") α form that changes into a [body-centered cubic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body-centered_cubic "Body-centered cubic") (lattice) β form at 882°C (1,620°F).[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p734-26)[[26]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-schmidt65-27) The [specific heat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Specific_heat_capacity "Specific heat capacity") of the α form increases dramatically as it is heated to this transition temperature but then falls and remains fairly constant for the β form regardless of temperature.[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p734-26)
### Chemical properties
[![Graph showing the Pourbaix diagram of titanium](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Titanium_in_water_Pourbaix_diagram.png/250px-Titanium_in_water_Pourbaix_diagram.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Titanium_in_water_Pourbaix_diagram.png)[Pourbaix diagram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pourbaix_diagram "Pourbaix diagram") for titanium in pure water, perchloric acid, or sodium hydroxide[[27]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-medusa-28)
Like [aluminium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aluminium "Aluminium") and [magnesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnesium "Magnesium"), the surface of titanium metal and its alloys [oxidizes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxidizes "Oxidizes") immediately upon exposure to air to form a thin non-porous [passivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passivation_\(chemistry\) "Passivation \(chemistry\)") layer that protects the bulk metal from further oxidation or corrosion.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EBC-13) When it first forms, this protective layer is only 1–2[nm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanometre "Nanometre") thick, but it continues to grow slowly, reaching a thickness of 25nm in four years.[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p453-29) This layer gives titanium excellent resistance to corrosion against oxidizing acids, but it will dissolve in dilute [hydrofluoric acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrofluoric_acid "Hydrofluoric acid"), hot hydrochloric acid, and hot sulfuric acid.[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:0-30)
Titanium is capable of withstanding attack by dilute [sulfuric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfuric_acid "Sulfuric acid") and [hydrochloric acids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrochloric_acid "Hydrochloric acid") at room temperature, chloride solutions, and most organic acids.[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14) However, titanium is corroded by concentrated acids.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-31) Titanium burns in normal air at temperatures lower than its melting point, so melting the metal is possible only in an inert atmosphere or vacuum.[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14) At room temperature, titanium is fairly inert to [halogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halogen "Halogen"), but will violently combine with [chlorine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chlorine "Chlorine") and [bromine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bromine "Bromine") at 550°C (1,022°F) to form [titanium tetrachloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_tetrachloride "Titanium tetrachloride") and [titanium tetrabromide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_tetrabromide "Titanium tetrabromide"), respectively.[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:0-30)
Titanium readily reacts with oxygen at 1,200°C (2,190°F) in air, and at 610°C (1,130°F) in pure oxygen, forming [titanium dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_dioxide "Titanium dioxide").[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-TICE6th-19) This oxide is also formed by reaction between titanium and pure oxygen at room temperature and pressure of 25 bars (2,500kPa).[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:0-30) Titanium is one of the few elements that burns in pure nitrogen gas, reacting at 800°C (1,470°F) to form [titanium nitride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_nitride "Titanium nitride"), which causes embrittlement.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-titaniumindustry-32)
### Occurrence
Titanium is the ninth-most [abundant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abundance_of_elements_in_Earth's_crust "Abundance of elements in Earth's crust") element in [Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth "Earth")'s crust (0.63% by [mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass "Mass"))[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p732-33) and the seventh-most abundant metal. It is present as oxides in most [igneous rocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igneous_rock "Igneous rock"), in [sediments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedimentary_rock "Sedimentary rock") derived from them, in living things, and natural bodies of water.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EBC-13)[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14) Of the 801 types of igneous rocks analyzed by the [United States Geological Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Geological_Survey "United States Geological Survey"), 784 contained titanium. Its proportion in soils is approximately 0.5–1.5%.[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p732-33)
Common titanium-containing [minerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral "Mineral") are [anatase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatase "Anatase"), [brookite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookite "Brookite"), [ilmenite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilmenite "Ilmenite"), [perovskite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perovskite "Perovskite"), [rutile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutile "Rutile"), and [titanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanite "Titanite") (sphene).[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p453-29) [Akaogiite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akaogiite "Akaogiite") is an extremely rare mineral consisting of titanium dioxide. Of these minerals, only rutile and ilmenite have economic importance, yet even they are difficult to find in high concentrations. About 6.0 and 0.7 million tonnes of those minerals were mined in 2011, respectively.[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-USGS-34) Significant titanium-bearing ilmenite deposits exist in [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia "Australia"), [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada "Canada"), [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China"), [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India "India"), [Mozambique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mozambique "Mozambique"), [New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand "New Zealand"), [Norway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norway "Norway"), [Sierra Leone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierra_Leone "Sierra Leone"), [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa "South Africa"), and [Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine "Ukraine").[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p453-29) Total reserves of anatase, ilmenite, and rutile are estimated to exceed 2 billion tonnes.[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-USGS-34)
The concentration of titanium is about 4 [picomolar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molar_concentration "Molar concentration") in the ocean. At 100°C, the concentration of titanium in water is estimated to be less than 10−7 M at pH 7. The identity of titanium species in aqueous solution remains unknown because of its low solubility and the lack of sensitive spectroscopic methods, although only the 4+ oxidation state is stable in air. No evidence exists for a biological role, although rare organisms are known to accumulate high concentrations of titanium.[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-35)
Titanium is contained in [meteorites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteorite "Meteorite"), and it has been detected in the [Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun "Sun") and in [M-type](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_classification "Stellar classification") [stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star "Star")[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14) (the coolest type) with a surface temperature of 3,200°C (5,790°F).[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p451-36) [Rocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_\(geology\) "Rock \(geology\)") brought back from the [Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon "Moon") during the [Apollo 17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_17 "Apollo 17") mission are composed of 12.1% TiO2.[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14) [Native](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_element_mineral "Native element mineral") titanium is only found in rocks that have been exposed to pressures between roughly 2.8 to 4.0[gigapascal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gigapascal "Gigapascal") on Earth,[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-37) but it has been identified in [nanocrystals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanocrystalline_material "Nanocrystalline material") on the Moon.[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-38)
### Isotopes
Main article: [Isotopes of titanium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_titanium "Isotopes of titanium")
Naturally occurring titanium is composed of five stable [isotopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotope "Isotope"): 46Ti, 47Ti, 48Ti, 49Ti, and 50Ti, with 48Ti being the most abundant (73.8% [natural abundance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_abundance "Natural abundance")). Twenty-three [radioisotopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioisotope "Radioisotope") have been characterized,[[b]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-40) the most stable of which are [44Ti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium-44 "Titanium-44") with a [half-life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-life "Half-life") of 63 years; 45Ti, 184.8 minutes; 51Ti, 5.76 minutes; and 52Ti, 1.7 minutes. All other [radioactive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive "Radioactive") isotopes have half-lives less than 33 seconds, with the majority less than half a second.[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EnvChem-20)[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-NUBASE2020-12)
The isotopes of titanium range from 39Ti to 66Ti.[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-AME2016_II-41)[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Tarasov2025-39) The primary [decay mode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decay_mode "Decay mode") for isotopes lighter than 46Ti is [positron emission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positron_emission "Positron emission") (with the exception of 44Ti, which undergoes [electron capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_capture "Electron capture")), leading to [isotopes of scandium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_scandium "Isotopes of scandium"), and the primary mode for isotopes heavier than 50Ti is [beta emission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_emission "Beta emission"), leading to [isotopes of vanadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_vanadium "Isotopes of vanadium").[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EnvChem-20) Titanium becomes radioactive upon bombardment with [deuterons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deuterons "Deuterons"), emitting mainly [positrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positrons "Positrons") and hard [gamma rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma_rays "Gamma rays").[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14)
## Compounds
See also the categories [Titanium compounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Titanium_compounds "Category:Titanium compounds") and [Titanium minerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Titanium_minerals "Category:Titanium minerals")
See also: [Titanium alloy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_alloy "Titanium alloy")
[![A steel colored twist drill bit with the spiral groove colored in a golden shade](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Titanium_nitride_coating.jpg/60px-Titanium_nitride_coating.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Titanium_nitride_coating.jpg)A titanium nitride-coated [drill bit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drill_bit "Drill bit")
The +4 [oxidation state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxidation_state "Oxidation state") dominates titanium chemistry,[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Greenwood1997p958-42) but compounds in the +3 oxidation state are also numerous.[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Greenwood1997p970-43) Commonly, titanium adopts an [octahedral coordination geometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octahedral_coordination_geometry "Octahedral coordination geometry") in its complexes,[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Greenwood1997p960-44)[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Greenwood1997p967-45) but tetrahedral TiCl4 is a notable exception. Because of its high oxidation state, titanium(IV) compounds exhibit a high degree of [covalent bonding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covalent_bond "Covalent bond").[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Greenwood1997p958-42)
### Oxides, sulfides, and alkoxides
The most important oxide is TiO2, which exists in three important [polymorphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polymorphism_\(materials_science\) "Polymorphism \(materials science\)"); anatase, brookite, and rutile. All three are white diamagnetic solids, although mineral samples can appear dark, as in [rutile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutile "Rutile"). They adopt polymeric structures in which Ti is surrounded by six [oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxide "Oxide") ligands that link to other Ti centers.[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Greenwood1997p961-46)
The term _[titanates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanate "Titanate")_ usually refers to titanium(IV) compounds, as represented by [barium titanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barium_titanate "Barium titanate") (BaTiO3). With a perovskite structure, this material exhibits [piezoelectric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piezoelectric "Piezoelectric") properties and is used as a transducer in the interconversion of [sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sound "Sound") and [electricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity "Electricity").[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-TICE6th-19) Many minerals are titanates, such as ilmenite (FeTiO3). [Star sapphires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_sapphire_\(jewel\) "Star sapphire \(jewel\)") and [rubies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruby "Ruby") get their [asterism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asterism_\(gemmology\) "Asterism \(gemmology\)") (star-forming shine) from the presence of titanium dioxide impurities.[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p453-29)
A variety of reduced oxides ([suboxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suboxide "Suboxide")) of titanium are known, mainly reduced [stoichiometries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoichiometry "Stoichiometry") of titanium dioxide obtained by [atmospheric plasma spraying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_plasma_spraying "Atmospheric plasma spraying"). Ti3O5, described as a Ti(IV)-Ti(III) species, is a purple semiconductor produced by [reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reduction_\(chemistry\) "Reduction \(chemistry\)") of TiO2 with hydrogen at high temperatures,[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-47) and is used industrially when surfaces need to be vapor-coated with titanium dioxide: it evaporates as pure TiO, whereas TiO2 evaporates as a mixture of oxides and deposits coatings with variable refractive index.[[46]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-48) Also known is [Ti2O3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium\(III\)_oxide "Titanium\(III\) oxide"), with the [corundum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corundum "Corundum") structure, and [TiO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium\(II\)_oxide "Titanium\(II\) oxide"), with the [rock salt structure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_salt_structure "Rock salt structure"), although often [nonstoichiometric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonstoichiometric "Nonstoichiometric").[[47]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreenwoodEarnshaw1997962-49)
The [alkoxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkoxide "Alkoxide") of titanium(IV), prepared by treating TiCl4 with [alcohols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_\(chemistry\) "Alcohol \(chemistry\)"), are colorless compounds that convert to the dioxide on reaction with water. They are industrially useful for depositing solid TiO2 via the [sol-gel process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sol-gel_process "Sol-gel process"). [Titanium isopropoxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_isopropoxide "Titanium isopropoxide") is used in the synthesis of chiral organic compounds via the [Sharpless epoxidation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharpless_epoxidation "Sharpless epoxidation").[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-50)
Titanium forms a variety of sulfides, but only [TiS2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_disulfide "Titanium disulfide") has attracted significant interest. It adopts a layered structure and was used as a cathode in the development of [lithium batteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_batteries "Lithium batteries"). Because Ti(IV) is a ["hard cation"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HSAB_theory "HSAB theory"), the sulfides of titanium are unstable and tend to hydrolyze to the oxide with release of [hydrogen sulfide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_sulfide "Hydrogen sulfide").[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-51)
### Nitrides and carbides
[Titanium nitride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_nitride "Titanium nitride") (TiN) is a refractory solid exhibiting extreme hardness, thermal/electrical conductivity, and a high melting point.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-52) TiN has a hardness equivalent to [sapphire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sapphire "Sapphire") and [carborundum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carborundum "Carborundum") (9.0 on the [Mohs scale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohs_scale "Mohs scale")),[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-53) and is often used to coat cutting tools, such as [drill bits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drill_bit "Drill bit").[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-54) It is also used as a gold-colored decorative finish and as a [barrier layer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper_interconnects#Barrier_metal "Copper interconnects") in [semiconductor fabrication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor_fabrication "Semiconductor fabrication").[[53]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-55) [Titanium carbide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_carbide "Titanium carbide") (TiC), which is also very hard, is found in cutting tools and coatings.[[54]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-56)
### Halides
[![Violet liquid in a test tube](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/TiCl3.jpg/250px-TiCl3.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TiCl3.jpg)Titanium(III) compounds are characteristically violet, illustrated by this aqueous solution of [titanium trichloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_trichloride "Titanium trichloride").
[Titanium tetrachloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_tetrachloride "Titanium tetrachloride") (titanium(IV) chloride, TiCl4[[55]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-57)) is a colorless volatile liquid (commercial samples are yellowish) that, in air, hydrolyzes with spectacular emission of white clouds. Via the [Kroll process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kroll_process "Kroll process"), TiCl4 is used in the conversion of titanium ores to titanium metal. Titanium tetrachloride is also used to make titanium dioxide, e.g., for use in white paint.[[56]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-58) It is widely used in [organic chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_chemistry "Organic chemistry") as a [Lewis acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_acids_and_bases "Lewis acids and bases"), for example in the [Mukaiyama aldol condensation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukaiyama_aldol_condensation "Mukaiyama aldol condensation").[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-59) In the [van Arkel–de Boer process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Arkel%E2%80%93de_Boer_process "Van Arkel–de Boer process"), [titanium tetraiodide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_tetraiodide "Titanium tetraiodide") (TiI4) is generated in the production of high purity titanium metal.[[58]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Greenwood1997p965-60)
Titanium(III) and titanium(II) also form stable chlorides. A notable example is [titanium(III) chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium\(III\)_chloride "Titanium\(III\) chloride") (TiCl3), which is used as a [catalyst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalyst "Catalyst") for production of [polyolefins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyolefin "Polyolefin") (see [Ziegler–Natta catalyst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziegler%E2%80%93Natta_catalyst "Ziegler–Natta catalyst")) and a reducing [agent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagent "Reagent") in organic chemistry.[[59]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-61)
### Organometallic complexes
Main article: [Organotitanium chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organotitanium_chemistry "Organotitanium chemistry")
Owing to the important role of titanium compounds as [polymerization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polymerization "Polymerization") catalyst, compounds with Ti-C bonds have been intensively studied. The most common organotitanium complex is [titanocene dichloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanocene_dichloride "Titanocene dichloride") ((C5H5)2TiCl2). Related compounds include [Tebbe's reagent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tebbe's_reagent "Tebbe's reagent") and [Petasis reagent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petasis_reagent "Petasis reagent"). Titanium forms [carbonyl complexes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metal_carbonyl "Metal carbonyl"), e.g. [(C5H5)2Ti(CO)2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanocene_dicarbonyl "Titanocene dicarbonyl").[[60]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-62)
## History
[![Engraved profile image of a mid-age male with high forehead. The person is wearing a coat and a neckerchief.](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Martin_Heinrich_Klaproth.jpg/250px-Martin_Heinrich_Klaproth.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Martin_Heinrich_Klaproth.jpg)[Martin Heinrich Klaproth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Heinrich_Klaproth "Martin Heinrich Klaproth") named titanium for the [Titans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_\(mythology\) "Titan \(mythology\)") of [Greek mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology "Greek mythology").
Titanium was [discovered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_of_the_chemical_elements "Discovery of the chemical elements") in 1791 by the [clergyman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clergy "Clergy") and [geologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geologist "Geologist") [William Gregor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Gregor "William Gregor") as an [inclusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inclusion_\(mineral\) "Inclusion \(mineral\)") of a mineral in [Cornwall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall "Cornwall"), Great Britain.[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p452-63) Gregor recognized the presence of a new element in ilmenite[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-HistoryAndUse-15) when he found black sand by a stream and noticed the sand was attracted by a [magnet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnet "Magnet").[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p452-63) Analyzing the sand, he determined the presence of two metal oxides: [iron oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_oxide "Iron oxide") (explaining the attraction to the magnet) and 45.25% of a white metallic oxide he could not identify.[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p732-33) Realizing that the unidentified oxide contained a metal that did not match any known element, in 1791 Gregor reported his findings in both German and French science journals: _[Crell's Annalen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crell's_Annalen "Crell's Annalen")_ and _Observations et Mémoires sur la Physique_.[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p452-63)[[62]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-64)[[63]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-65) He named this oxide [manaccanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manaccanite "Manaccanite").[[64]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-66)
Around the same time, [Franz-Joseph Müller von Reichenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz-Joseph_M%C3%BCller_von_Reichenstein "Franz-Joseph Müller von Reichenstein") produced a similar substance, but could not identify it.[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-HistoryAndUse-15) The oxide was independently rediscovered in 1795 by [Prussian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prussia "Prussia") chemist [Martin Heinrich Klaproth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Heinrich_Klaproth "Martin Heinrich Klaproth") in rutile from Boinik (the German name of Bajmócska), a village in Hungary (now [Bojničky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bojni%C4%8Dky "Bojničky") in Slovakia).[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p452-63)[[c]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-68) Klaproth found that it contained a new element and named it for the [Titans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_\(mythology\) "Titan \(mythology\)") of [Greek mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology "Greek mythology").[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p451-36) After hearing about Gregor's earlier discovery, he obtained a sample of manaccanite and confirmed that it contained titanium.[[66]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-69)
The currently known processes for extracting titanium from its various ores are laborious and costly; it is not possible to reduce the ore by heating with [carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon "Carbon") (as in iron smelting) because titanium combines with the carbon to produce titanium carbide.[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p452-63) An extraction of 95% pure titanium was achieved by [Lars Fredrik Nilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lars_Fredrik_Nilson "Lars Fredrik Nilson") and [Otto Petterson](https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto%20Pettersson "it:Otto Pettersson"). To achieve this they chlorinated titanium oxide in a carbon monoxide atmosphere with chlorine gas before reducing it to titanium metal by the use of sodium.[[67]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-70) Pure metallic titanium (99.9%) was first prepared in 1910 by [Matthew A. Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_A._Hunter "Matthew A. Hunter") at [Rensselaer Polytechnic Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rensselaer_Polytechnic_Institute "Rensselaer Polytechnic Institute") by heating titanium tetrachloride (TiCl4) with [sodium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium "Sodium") at 700–800°C (1,292–1,472°F) under great pressure[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Roza2008p9-71) in a [batch process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batch_production "Batch production") known as the [Hunter process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_process "Hunter process").[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14) Titanium metal was not used outside the laboratory until 1932 when [William Justin Kroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Justin_Kroll "William Justin Kroll") produced it by reducing TiCl4 with [calcium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium "Calcium").[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Greenwood1997p955-72) Eight years later he refined this process with magnesium and with sodium in what became known as the [Kroll process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kroll_process "Kroll process").[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Greenwood1997p955-72) Although research continues to seek cheaper and more efficient routes, such as the [FFC Cambridge process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FFC_Cambridge_process "FFC Cambridge process"), the Kroll process is still predominantly used for commercial production.[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14)[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-HistoryAndUse-15)
[![Rough pieces of shiny metal](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Titanium_metal.jpg/250px-Titanium_metal.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Titanium_metal.jpg)Titanium "sponge", made by the [Kroll process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kroll_process "Kroll process")
Titanium of very high purity was made in small quantities when [Anton Eduard van Arkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Eduard_van_Arkel "Anton Eduard van Arkel") and [Jan Hendrik de Boer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Hendrik_de_Boer "Jan Hendrik de Boer") discovered the iodide process in 1925, by reacting with iodine and decomposing the formed vapors over a hot filament to pure metal.[[70]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-73)
In the 1950s and 1960s, the Soviet Union pioneered the use of titanium in military and submarine applications[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Roza2008p9-71) ([Alfa class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfa-class_submarine "Alfa-class submarine"), [Mike class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_submarine_K-278_Komsomolets "Soviet submarine K-278 Komsomolets") and [Sierra II class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierra-class_submarine "Sierra-class submarine"))[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-74) as part of programs related to the Cold War.[[72]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-75) Starting in the early 1950s, titanium came into use extensively in military aviation, particularly in high-performance jets, starting with aircraft such as the [F-100 Super Sabre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-100_Super_Sabre "F-100 Super Sabre") and [Lockheed A-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_A-12 "Lockheed A-12") and [SR-71](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SR-71 "SR-71").[[73]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-76)
Throughout the Cold War period, titanium was considered a [strategic material](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_material "Strategic material") by the U.S. government, and a large stockpile of titanium [sponge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metal_foam "Metal foam") (a porous form of the pure metal) was maintained by the [Defense National Stockpile Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_National_Stockpile_Center "Defense National Stockpile Center"), until the stockpile was dispersed in the 2000s.[[74]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-77) Even so, the U.S. government annually allocates 15,000metric tons of titanium sponge as potential acquisitions for the stockpile.[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:3-78)
## Production
Main article: [Titanium production by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_production_by_country "Titanium production by country")
