Titanium production is largely divided into three measured categories: manufacture of porous titanium metal "sponge", titanium oxide pigment, and titanium mineral concentrates used for the production of sponge, pigment, metal ingots, and other titanium products such as coatings. These concentrates are largely made up of the mineral [ilmenite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilmenite "Ilmenite"), but also include [anatase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatase "Anatase"), natural and synthetic [rutile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutile "Rutile"), [tailings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tailings "Tailings"), [slag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slag "Slag"), and [leucoxene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leucoxene "Leucoxene"). As of 2024, the largest producers of titanium mineral concentrates were [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China"), [Mozambique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mozambique "Mozambique"), and [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa "South Africa").[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:3-78)
Most of the world's titanium is produced in China. The [United States Geological Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Geological_Survey "United States Geological Survey")'s 2025 report on mineral commodities estimated that out of the 320,000 metric tons (310,000 long tons) of titanium sponge produced globally in 2024, 220,000 (69%) were produced in China, with the second-largest producer being [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan "Japan") (which produced 55,000metric tons in the same year, 17% of the total). Japan was the largest exporter of titanium sponge in 2024, but did not produce any titanium minerals on its own.[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:3-78) A prior report in 2021 noted that the four leading producers of titanium sponge were China (52%), Japan (24%), Russia (16%) and Kazakhstan (7%).[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-USGS-34) Russia remains the third-largest producer of titanium sponge[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:3-78) through the efforts of the metallurgy company [VSMPO-AVISMA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VSMPO-AVISMA "VSMPO-AVISMA"), despite [international sanctions during the Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine "International sanctions during the Russian invasion of Ukraine").[[76]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-81) Production statistics on titanium dioxide pigment are not as clear-cut, but estimates placed the maximum capacity on global pigment production at 9,800,000 metric tons (9,600,000 long tons) in 2024.[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:3-78)
Various methods have been developed to extract and refine titanium from ore since the metal was first purified in 1910.[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:0-30)[[77]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:4-82)
### Mineral beneficiation processes
[![A small heap of uniform black grains smaller than 1mm in diameter](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/TitaniumUSGOV.jpg/250px-TitaniumUSGOV.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TitaniumUSGOV.jpg)Mineral concentrate of fine-grained titanium
Several processes have been developed to extract titanium and usable titanium-containing minerals from ore. The [Becher process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Becher_process "Becher process") is an industrial process used to produce synthetic rutile, a form of titanium dioxide, from the ore ilmenite by removing iron.[[78]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-83) It is not used at scale.[[77]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:4-82) The [chloride process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chloride_process "Chloride process") produces titanium tetrachloride through treatment of rutile ore with chlorine and carbon at high heat,[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Greenwood1997p960-44) then oxidizes the product with an oxygen flame or plasma to produce titanium dioxide.[[79]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-UllmannPigments-84) The [sulfate process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_dioxide#Sulfate_process "Titanium dioxide") uses [sulfuric acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfuric_acid "Sulfuric acid") (H2SO4) to leach titanium from ilmenite ore (FeTiO3), producing [titanyl sulfate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanyl_sulfate "Titanyl sulfate") (TiOSO4). This sulfate is broken into two hydrates, TiO2 and H2SO4, through addition of water, and this water is removed by adding heat, which produces titanium dioxide as the end product.[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-bar1-85)
### Purification processes
See also: [Category:Titanium processes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Titanium_processes "Category:Titanium processes") and [Category:Titanium companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Titanium_companies "Category:Titanium companies")
#### Hunter process
The Hunter process was the first industrial process to produce pure metallic titanium. It was invented in 1910 by [Matthew A. Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_A._Hunter "Matthew A. Hunter"), a [chemist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemist "Chemist") born in New Zealand who worked in the United States.[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-86) The process involves reducing [titanium tetrachloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_tetrachloride "Titanium tetrachloride") (TiCl4) with [sodium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium "Sodium") (Na) in a batch reactor with an inert atmosphere at a temperature of 1,000°C. Dilute [hydrochloric acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrochloric_acid "Hydrochloric acid") is then used to leach the salt from the product.[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-87)     TiCl4(g) + 4 Na(l) → 4 NaCl(l) + Ti(s)
#### Kroll process
[![Slightly yellow liquid in a vial with a white cap on a black table](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Sample_of_Titanium_tetrachloride_01.jpg/250px-Sample_of_Titanium_tetrachloride_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sample_of_Titanium_tetrachloride_01.jpg)Sample of titanium tetrachloride, a volatile liquid
The processing of titanium metal occurs in four major steps: reduction of titanium ore into "sponge", a porous form; melting of sponge, or sponge plus a master alloy to form an ingot; primary fabrication, where an ingot is converted into general mill products such as [billet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_stock "Bar stock"), bar, [plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_\(metal\) "Plate \(metal\)"), [sheet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheet_metal "Sheet metal"), strip, and [tube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tube_\(fluid_conveyance\) "Tube \(fluid conveyance\)"); and secondary fabrication of finished shapes from mill products.[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-88)
Because it cannot be readily produced by reduction of titanium dioxide,[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Stwertka1998-21) titanium metal is obtained by reduction of [titanium tetrachloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_tetrachloride "Titanium tetrachloride") (TiCl4) with magnesium metal in the Kroll process. The complexity of this batch production in the Kroll process explains the relatively high market value of titanium,[[84]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p733-89) despite the Kroll process being less expensive than the Hunter process.[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Roza2008p9-71) To produce the TiCl4 required by the Kroll process, the dioxide is subjected to [carbothermic reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbothermic_reduction "Carbothermic reduction") in the presence of [chlorine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chlorine "Chlorine"). In this process, the chlorine gas is passed over a red-hot mixture of rutile or ilmenite in the presence of carbon. After extensive purification by [fractional distillation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fractional_distillation "Fractional distillation"), the TiCl4 is reduced with 800°C (1,470°F) molten magnesium in an [argon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argon "Argon") atmosphere.[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-TICE6th-19)      2 FeTiO 3 + 7 Cl 2 + 6 C → 900 o C 2 FeCl 3 + 2 TiCl 4 + 6 CO {\displaystyle {\ce {2FeTiO3 + 7Cl2 + 6C ->[900^oC] 2FeCl3 + 2TiCl4 + 6CO}}} ![{\\displaystyle {2\\,\\mathrm {FeTiO} {\\vphantom {A}}_{\\smash\[{t}\]{3}}{}+{}7\\,\\mathrm {Cl} {\\vphantom {A}}_{\\smash\[{t}\]{2}}{}+{}6\\,\\mathrm {C} {}\\mathrel {\\xrightarrow {900{\\vphantom {A}}^{o}\\mathrm {C} } } {}2\\,\\mathrm {FeCl} {\\vphantom {A}}_{\\smash\[{t}\]{3}}{}+{}2\\,\\mathrm {TiCl} {\\vphantom {A}}_{\\smash\[{t}\]{4}}{}+{}6\\,\\mathrm {CO} }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/1eeb2d68819d30ab4f11311db1e92a04acc56bc6)      TiCl 4 + 2 Mg → 1100 o C Ti + 2 MgCl 2 {\displaystyle {\ce {TiCl4 + 2Mg ->[1100^oC] Ti + 2MgCl2}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\mathrm {TiCl} {\\vphantom {A}}_{\\smash\[{t}\]{4}}{}+{}2\\,\\mathrm {Mg} {}\\mathrel {\\xrightarrow {1100{\\vphantom {A}}^{o}\\mathrm {C} } } {}\\mathrm {Ti} {}+{}2\\,\\mathrm {MgCl} {\\vphantom {A}}_{\\smash\[{t}\]{2}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f228cf9d1b37bed2a16479329a0cbac935343617)
#### Arkel-Boer process
The [van Arkel–de Boer process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Arkel%E2%80%93de_Boer_process "Van Arkel–de Boer process") was the first semi-industrial process developed to produce pure titanium, invented by [Anton Eduard van Arkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Eduard_van_Arkel "Anton Eduard van Arkel") and [Jan Hendrik de Boer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Hendrik_de_Boer "Jan Hendrik de Boer") in 1925 for the electronics company [Philips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philips_Nv "Philips Nv").[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:1-90) It is a closed-loop process[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:2-91) that involves thermal decomposition of [titanium tetraiodide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_tetraiodide "Titanium tetraiodide").[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-92) This same process is used to purify other metals, such as thorium, hafnium, and zirconium,[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:1-90) and a similar process using further refined iodide was used to refine chromium. A desire to develop processes that could be run continuously led to the development of commercial processes to refine titanium.[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:2-91)
#### Armstrong process
[Titanium powder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_powder "Titanium powder") is manufactured using a [flow production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flow_production "Flow production") process known as the [Armstrong process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armstrong_process "Armstrong process")[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Roza2008p25-93) that is similar to the batch production [Hunter process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_process "Hunter process"). A stream of titanium tetrachloride gas is added to a stream of molten sodium; the products (sodium chloride salt and titanium particles) are filtered from the extra sodium. Titanium is then separated from the salt by water washing. Both the sodium and chlorine are recycled to produce and process more titanium tetrachloride.[[89]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-ECI_online-94)
#### Other processes
The titanium tetrachloride used as an intermediate in both the Hunter and Kroll process is a volatile and corrosive liquid, and is thus hazardous to work with. The processes involving the tetrachloride, both its formation and the vacuum distillation processes used to purify the final material, are slow, and have prompted development of other techniques.[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-flower-95)
Methods for [electrolytic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrolytic "Electrolytic") production of Ti metal from TiO2 using molten salt electrolytes have been proposed starting in the 1990s,[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-flower-95) and have been researched and tested at laboratory and small pilot plant scales.[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-fray17-96) While some metals such as [nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel "Nickel") and [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper "Copper") can be refined by [electrowinning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrowinning "Electrowinning") at room temperature, titanium must be in the molten state, which is likely to damage the [refractory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refractory "Refractory") lining of a reaction vessel.[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-sohn23-97) Zhang and colleagues concluded in 2017 that despite industry interests in finding new ways to manufacture titanium metal, no method had yet been developed to commercially replace the Kroll process.[[93]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-zhang17-98) One manufacturer in Virginia has developed a method to recycle scrap titanium metal back into powder, though their scale remains small, having the goal of producing only 125tons of titanium per year as of 2025.[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-:3-78)
One method that has been developed to potentially supplant the Kroll process is known as [hydrogen-assisted magnesiothermic reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_assisted_magnesiothermic_reduction "Hydrogen assisted magnesiothermic reduction") and makes use of [magnesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnesium "Magnesium"), hydrochloric acid, and a hydrogen atmosphere to directly reduce titanium dioxide to pure titanium. The reduction of titanium dioxide powder by magnesium in an atomphere of hydrogen can be followed by a leaching step with hydrochloric acid, which removes magnesium and residual non-titanium oxides. This is followed by additional reduction and leaching steps, and eventually results in pure titanium powder or [titanium hydride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_hydride "Titanium hydride").[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-99)
### Fabrication
All [welding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welding "Welding") of titanium must be done in an inert atmosphere of argon or [helium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helium "Helium") to shield it from contamination with atmospheric gases (oxygen, nitrogen, and hydrogen).[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p734-26) Contamination causes a variety of conditions, such as [embrittlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embrittlement "Embrittlement"), which reduce the integrity of the assembly welds and lead to joint failure.[[95]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-100)
Titanium is very difficult to [solder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solder "Solder") directly, and hence a [solderable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solderability "Solderability") metal or alloy such as steel is coated on titanium prior to soldering.[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-101) Titanium metal can be machined with the same equipment and the same processes as [stainless steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stainless_steel "Stainless steel").[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Barksdale1968p734-26)
#### Titanium alloys
Main article: [Titanium alloys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_alloys "Titanium alloys")
[![Silvery metal items, including rods, a tube, and powder in plastic containers, all resting on a reflective metal plate](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/Titanium_products.jpg/250px-Titanium_products.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Titanium_products.jpg)Basic titanium products: plate, tube, rods, and powder
Common [titanium alloys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_alloy "Titanium alloy") are made by reduction. For example, cuprotitanium (rutile with [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper "Copper") added), ferrocarbon titanium (ilmenite reduced with [coke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coke_\(fuel\) "Coke \(fuel\)") in an electric furnace), and manganotitanium (rutile with manganese or manganese oxides) are reduced.[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-TI_Encarta2005-102)
About fifty grades of [titanium alloys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_alloy "Titanium alloy") are designed and currently used, although only a couple of dozen are readily available commercially.[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-103) The [ASTM International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASTM_International "ASTM International") recognizes 31grades of titanium metal and alloys, of which grades one through four are commercially pure (unalloyed). Those four vary in tensile strength as a function of oxygen content, with grade 1 being the most ductile (lowest tensile strength with an oxygen content of 0.18%), and grade4 the least ductile (highest tensile strength with an oxygen content of 0.40%).[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p453-29) The remaining grades are alloys, each designed for specific properties of ductility, strength, hardness, electrical resistivity, [creep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creep_\(deformation\) "Creep \(deformation\)") resistance, specific corrosion resistance, and combinations thereof.[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-104)
In addition to the ASTM specifications, titanium alloys are also produced to meet aerospace and military specifications (SAE-AMS, MIL-T), ISO standards, and country-specific specifications, as well as proprietary end-user specifications for aerospace, military, medical, and industrial applications.[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-105)
#### Forming and forging
Commercially pure flat product (sheet, plate) can be formed readily, but processing must take into account of the tendency of the metal to [springback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springback "Springback"). This is especially true of certain high-strength alloys.[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-106)[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-107) Exposure to the oxygen in air at the elevated temperatures used in forging results in formation of a brittle oxygen-rich metallic surface layer called "[alpha case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_case "Alpha case")" that worsens the fatigue properties, so it must be removed by milling, etching, or electrochemical treatment.[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-"Chen_2001"-108) The working of titanium may include [friction welding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friction_welding "Friction welding"),[[104]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-mti1-109) [cryo-forging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryo-forging?action=edit&redlink=1 "Cryo-forging \(page does not exist\)"),[[105]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-mdes1-110) and [vacuum arc remelting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacuum_arc_remelting "Vacuum arc remelting").[[106]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-111)
## Applications
[![A hand holding a cylinder of reflective silvery metal](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Titanzylinder.jpg/250px-Titanzylinder.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Titanzylinder.jpg)A titanium cylinder
Titanium is used in steel as an alloying element ([ferro-titanium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferro-titanium "Ferro-titanium")) to reduce [grain size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystallite "Crystallite") and as a [deoxidizer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deoxidizer "Deoxidizer"), and in stainless steel to reduce carbon content.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EBC-13) Titanium is often alloyed with aluminium (to refine grain size), [vanadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanadium "Vanadium"), copper (to harden), [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron "Iron"), [manganese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manganese "Manganese"), [molybdenum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molybdenum "Molybdenum"), and other metals.[[107]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-ECE738-112) Titanium mill products (sheet, plate, bar, wire, forgings, castings) find application in industrial, aerospace, recreational, and emerging markets. Powdered titanium is used in [pyrotechnics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyrotechnics "Pyrotechnics") as a source of bright-burning particles.[[108]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-113)
### Pigments, additives, and coatings
[![White powder in a small pile](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Titanium-dioxide-sample.jpg/250px-Titanium-dioxide-sample.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Titanium-dioxide-sample.jpg)[Titanium dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_dioxide "Titanium dioxide") is the most commonly used compound of titanium.[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-USGS-34)
[Titanium dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_dioxide "Titanium dioxide") (TiO  
2) is the most common compound of the element, being the end point of 95% of the world's refined titanium. It is a widely used white [pigment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pigment "Pigment").[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-USGS-34) It is also used in cement, in gemstones, and as an optical opacifier in paper.[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-114)
TiO  
2 pigment is chemically inert, resists fading in sunlight, and is very opaque: it imparts a pure and brilliant white color to the brown or grey chemicals that form the majority of household plastics.[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-HistoryAndUse-15) In nature, this compound is found in the minerals anatase, brookite, and rutile.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-EBC-13) Paint made with titanium dioxide does well in severe temperatures and marine environments.[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-HistoryAndUse-15) Pure titanium dioxide has a very high [index of refraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refractive_index "Refractive index") and an [optical dispersion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optical_dispersion "Optical dispersion") higher than [diamond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diamond "Diamond").[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14) Titanium dioxide is used in [sunscreens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunscreen "Sunscreen") because it reflects and absorbs [UV light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UV_light "UV light").[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Stwertka1998-21)
### Aerospace and marine
[![A black aircraft in midair](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/A12-flying.jpg/250px-A12-flying.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:A12-flying.jpg)The [Lockheed A-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_A-12 "Lockheed A-12"), one of the first planes with a frame mostly made of titanium
Because titanium alloys have high [tensile strength](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tensile_strength "Tensile strength") to density ratio,[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-TICE6th-19) high [corrosion resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrosion_resistance "Corrosion resistance"),[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14) fatigue resistance, high crack resistance,[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Moiseyev-115) and ability to withstand moderately high temperatures without creeping, they are used in aircraft, armor plating, naval ships, spacecraft, and missiles.[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14)[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-HistoryAndUse-15) For these applications, titanium is alloyed with aluminium, zirconium, nickel,[[111]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Kramer-2013-07-05-116) vanadium, and other elements to manufacture a variety of components including critical structural parts, [landing gear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landing_gear "Landing gear"), [firewalls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firewall_\(engine\) "Firewall \(engine\)"), exhaust ducts (helicopters), and hydraulic systems. About two thirds of all titanium metal produced is used in aircraft frames and engines.[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p454-117) The [titanium 6AL-4V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_6AL-4V "Titanium 6AL-4V") alloy accounts for almost 50% of all alloys used in aircraft applications.[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-118)
The [Lockheed A-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_A-12 "Lockheed A-12") and the [SR-71 "Blackbird"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SR-71_Blackbird "SR-71 Blackbird") were two of the first aircraft frames where titanium was used, paving the way for much wider use in modern military and commercial aircraft. A large amount of titanium mill products are used in the production of many aircraft, such as (following values are amount of raw mill products used, only a fraction of this ends up in the finished aircraft): 116metric tons are used in the [Boeing 787](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_787 "Boeing 787"), 77 in the [Airbus A380](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A380 "Airbus A380"), 59 in the [Boeing 777](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_777 "Boeing 777"), 45 in the [Boeing 747](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_747 "Boeing 747"), 32 in the [Airbus A340](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A340 "Airbus A340"), 18 in the [Boeing 737](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_737 "Boeing 737"), 18 in the [Airbus A330](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A330 "Airbus A330"), and 12 in the [Airbus A320](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A320 "Airbus A320").[[114]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-119) In aero engine applications, titanium is used for rotors, compressor blades, hydraulic system components, and [nacelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacelle "Nacelle").[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-120)[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-121) An early use in jet engines was for the [Orenda Iroquois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orenda_Iroquois "Orenda Iroquois") in the 1950s.[[117]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-122)[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-123)[[119]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-124)
Because titanium is resistant to corrosion by sea water, it is used to make propeller shafts, rigging, [heat exchangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_exchanger "Heat exchanger") in [desalination plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desalination_plant "Desalination plant"),[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-LANL-14) heater-chillers for salt water aquariums, fishing line and leader, and divers' knives. Titanium is used in the housings and components of ocean-deployed surveillance and monitoring devices for science and military. The former [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") developed techniques for making submarines with hulls of titanium alloys,[[120]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-125) forging titanium in huge vacuum tubes.[[111]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Kramer-2013-07-05-116)
### Industrial
Welded titanium pipe and process equipment (heat exchangers, tanks, process vessels, valves) are used in the chemical and petrochemical industries primarily for corrosion resistance. Specific alloys are used in oil and gas downhole applications and [nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel "Nickel") [hydrometallurgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrometallurgy "Hydrometallurgy") for their high strength (e. g.: titanium beta C alloy), corrosion resistance, or both. The [pulp and paper industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulp_and_paper_industry "Pulp and paper industry") uses titanium in process equipment exposed to corrosive media, such as [sodium hypochlorite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_hypochlorite "Sodium hypochlorite") or wet chlorine gas (in the bleachery).[[121]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-126) Titanium is also used in [sputtering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputtering "Sputtering") targets.[[122]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-127)
Powdered titanium acts as a non-evaporative [getter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Getter "Getter"), and is one of several gas-reactive materials used to remove gases from [ultra-high vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultra-high_vacuum "Ultra-high vacuum") systems.[[123]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-128) This application manifested in [titanium sublimation pumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_sublimation_pump "Titanium sublimation pump")[[124]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-129) first employed in 1961,[[125]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-130) though the metal was first used in vacuum systems to prevent chambers from oxidizing in a design created by [Raymond Herb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_Herb "Raymond Herb") in 1953.[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-131)
Titanium tetrachloride (TiCl4), a colorless liquid, is important as an intermediate in the process of making TiO2 and is also used to produce the Ziegler–Natta catalyst. Titanium tetrachloride is also used to iridize glass and, because it fumes strongly in moist air, it is used to make smoke screens.[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Stwertka1998-21) In many industrial applications, titanium and its alloys can serve as a potential substitute for other metals, such as nickel, niobium, scandium, silver, tantalum, and tungsten.[[127]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-132)
### Consumer and architectural
[![Loudspeaker box](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Tweeter_with_Titanium_membrane_of_loudspeaker_box_JBL_TI_5000%2C_1990s.jpg/250px-Tweeter_with_Titanium_membrane_of_loudspeaker_box_JBL_TI_5000%2C_1990s.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tweeter_with_Titanium_membrane_of_loudspeaker_box_JBL_TI_5000,_1990s.jpg)[Tweeter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tweeter "Tweeter") loudspeaker driver with a membrane with 25 mm diameter made from titanium; from a [JBL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JBL "JBL") TI 5000 [loudspeaker box](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loudspeaker_box "Loudspeaker box"), c.1997
Titanium metal is used in automotive applications, particularly in automobile and motorcycle racing where low weight and high strength and rigidity are critical.[[128]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-133) The metal is generally too expensive for the general consumer market, though some late model [Corvettes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevrolet_Corvette "Chevrolet Corvette") have been manufactured with titanium exhausts.[[129]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-134)
Titanium is used in many sporting goods: tennis rackets, golf clubs, lacrosse stick shafts; cricket, hockey, lacrosse, and football helmet grills, and some bicycle frames and components.[[130]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-135) Titanium is used in spectacle frames, as it is durable and protect the lenses, though it may be less flexible than alternatives.[[131]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-136) Its [biocompatibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_biocompatibility "Titanium biocompatibility") is a potential benefit over other glasses frame materials.[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-137) Titanium is a common material for backpacking cookware and eating utensils. Titanium horseshoes are preferred to steel by [farriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farrier "Farrier") because they are lighter and more durable.[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Donachie2000-138) Some upmarket lightweight and corrosion-resistant tools, such as shovels, knife handles and flashlights, are made of titanium or titanium alloys.[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-use-139)
[![Building made of abstract shapes covered in silvery metal](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/El_Guggenheim_vizca%C3%ADno._%281454058701%29.jpg/250px-El_Guggenheim_vizca%C3%ADno._%281454058701%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:El_Guggenheim_vizca%C3%ADno._\(1454058701\).jpg)Titanium cladding of [Frank Gehry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Gehry "Frank Gehry")'s [Guggenheim Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guggenheim_Museum_Bilbao "Guggenheim Museum Bilbao"), [Bilbao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilbao "Bilbao")
Titanium has occasionally been used in architecture. The 42.5m (139ft) [Monument to Yuri Gagarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_to_Yuri_Gagarin "Monument to Yuri Gagarin"), the first man to travel in space, as well as the upper part of the 110m (360ft) [Monument to the Conquerors of Space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_to_the_Conquerors_of_Space "Monument to the Conquerors of Space") on top of the [Cosmonaut Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Museum_of_Cosmonautics "Memorial Museum of Cosmonautics") in Moscow are made of titanium.[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-140)[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-141) The [Guggenheim Museum Bilbao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guggenheim_Museum_Bilbao "Guggenheim Museum Bilbao") and the [Cerritos Millennium Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerritos_Millennium_Library "Cerritos Millennium Library") were the first buildings in Europe and North America, respectively, to be sheathed in titanium panels.[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p454-117) Titanium sheathing was used in the Frederic C. Hamilton Building in Denver, Colorado.[[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-142)
Because of titanium's superior strength and light weight relative to other metals (steel, stainless steel, and aluminium), and because of advances in metalworking techniques, its use has become widespread in the manufacture of firearms. Primary uses include pistol frames and revolver cylinders. For the same reasons, it is used in the body of some laptop computers (for example, in [Apple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_Inc. "Apple Inc.")'s [PowerBook G4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PowerBook_G4 "PowerBook G4"))[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-143)[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-use-139) and phones (such as the [iPhone 15 Pro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone_15_Pro "IPhone 15 Pro")).[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-144)
### Jewelry
[![Diagram showing different titanium colors in relation to the voltage that produced](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Anodized_titanium_table.jpg/250px-Anodized_titanium_table.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Anodized_titanium_table.jpg)Relation between voltage and color for anodized titanium
Because of its durability, titanium is used in some designer jewelry, such as [titanium rings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_ring "Titanium ring").[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Donachie2000-138) Its inertness makes it hypoallergenic and wearable in environments such as swimming pools. Titanium is also [alloyed with gold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_gold "Titanium gold") to produce an alloy that can be marketed as [24-karat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fineness "Fineness") gold, because the 1% of alloyed Ti is insufficient to require a lesser mark. The resulting alloy is roughly the hardness of 14-karat gold and is more durable than pure 24-karat gold.[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-145)
Titanium's durability, light weight, and dent and corrosion resistance make it useful for [watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watch "Watch") cases.[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Donachie2000-138) Some artists work with titanium to produce sculptures, decorative objects and furniture.[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-146) Titanium may be [anodized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anodising "Anodising") to vary the thickness of the surface oxide layer, causing optical [interference fringes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interference_fringe "Interference fringe") and a variety of bright colors.[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-147) With its variable coloration and chemical inertness, titanium is a popular metal for [body piercing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_piercing "Body piercing").[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-148)
Titanium has a minor use in dedicated non-circulating coins and medals. In 1999, the world's first titanium coin was minted for [Gibraltar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibraltar "Gibraltar")'s millennium celebration.[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-149) Pobjoy Mint, the British mint that produced the coin, continued to manufacture anodized titanium coins[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-150) until its closure in 2023.[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-151) The [Gold Coast Titans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_Coast_Titans "Gold Coast Titans"), an Australian rugby league team, award a medal of pure titanium to their player of the year.[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-152)
### Medical
Main article: [Titanium biocompatibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_biocompatibility "Titanium biocompatibility")
Because titanium is [biocompatible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biocompatibility "Biocompatibility") (non-toxic and not rejected by the body), it has many medical uses, including surgical implements and implants, such as hip balls and sockets ([joint replacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_replacement "Joint replacement")) and [dental implants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dental_implant "Dental implant").[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p452-63) Titanium and titanium alloy implants have been used in surgery since the 1950s, and are favored due to their low rate of corrosion, long life, and low [Young's modulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young's_modulus "Young's modulus"). A titanium alloy that contains 6% aluminium and 4% vanadium commonly used in the aerospace industry is also a common material for artificial joints.[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-e429-153)
[![Screws and plate with ruler](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Titanium_plaatje_voor_pols.jpg/250px-Titanium_plaatje_voor_pols.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Titanium_plaatje_voor_pols.jpg)Medical screws and plate used to repair wrist fractures. Scale is in centimeters. 
Titanium has the inherent ability to [osseointegrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osseointegration "Osseointegration"), enabling use in [dental implants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dental_implants "Dental implants") that can last for over 30 years. This property is also useful for [orthopedic implant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_fixator "Internal fixator") applications.[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p452-63) These benefit from titanium's lower modulus of elasticity to more closely match that of the bone that such devices are intended to repair. As a result, skeletal loads are more evenly shared between bone and implant, leading to a lower incidence of bone degradation due to stress shielding and [periprosthetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Periprosthetic "Periprosthetic") bone fractures, which occur at the boundaries of orthopedic implants. However, titanium alloys' stiffness is still more than twice that of bone, so adjacent bone bears a greatly reduced load and may deteriorate.[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-154)[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-155) Biomedical implants coated with a combination of silver and titanium have been researched as a potential option for load-bearing implants that need antimicrobial surfaces.[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-e429-153)
Modern advancements in additive manufacturing techniques have increased potential for titanium use in orthopedic implant applications.[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-156) Complex implant scaffold designs can be 3D-printed using titanium alloys, which allows for more patient-specific applications and increased implant osseointegration.[[152]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-157) Because titanium is non-[ferromagnetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferromagnetic "Ferromagnetic"), patients with titanium implants can be safely examined with [magnetic resonance imaging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic_resonance_imaging "Magnetic resonance imaging") (convenient for long-term implants). Preparing titanium for implantation in the body involves subjecting it to a high-temperature [plasma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasma_\(physics\) "Plasma \(physics\)") arc which removes the surface atoms, exposing fresh titanium that is instantly oxidized.[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p452-63) Titanium is used for the [surgical instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surgical_instrument "Surgical instrument") used in [image-guided surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Image-guided_surgery "Image-guided surgery"), as well as wheelchairs, crutches, and any other products where high strength and low weight are desirable.[[153]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-158)
Titanium dioxide [nanoparticles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanoparticle "Nanoparticle") are widely used in electronics and the delivery of [pharmaceuticals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical_drug "Pharmaceutical drug") and cosmetics.[[154]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-159)
#### Anticancer therapy studies
Following the success of [platinum-based](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cisplatin "Cisplatin") chemotherapy, titanium(IV) complexes were among the first non-platinum compounds to be tested and accepted for clinical trials in cancer treatment.[[155]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-160) The advantage of titanium compounds lies in their high efficacy and low toxicity _[in vivo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_vivo "In vivo")_. In biological environments, hydrolysis leads to the safe and inert titanium dioxide. Despite these advantages, the first candidate compounds failed clinical trials due to insufficient efficacy to toxicity ratios and formulation complications. Further development resulted in the creation of potentially effective, selective, and stable titanium-based drugs.[[156]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Tshuva-Miller-161)
### Nuclear waste storage
Because of its corrosion resistance, containers made of titanium have been studied for the long-term storage of nuclear waste. Containers lasting more than 100,000 years are thought possible with manufacturing conditions that minimize material defects.[[157]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-162) A titanium "drip shield" has been considered for installation over containers of other types to enhance their longevity.[[158]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-163)
## Hazards and safety  
Titanium (powder)  
| Hazards  |  
| --- |  
|  [**GHS** labelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globally_Harmonized_System_of_Classification_and_Labelling_of_Chemicals "Globally Harmonized System of Classification and Labelling of Chemicals"):  |  
| [Pictograms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GHS_hazard_pictograms "GHS hazard pictograms")  | [![GHS02: Flammable](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/GHS-pictogram-flamme.svg/60px-GHS-pictogram-flamme.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:GHS-pictogram-flamme.svg "GHS02: Flammable")  |  
| [Signal word](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globally_Harmonized_System_of_Classification_and_Labelling_of_Chemicals#Signal_word "Globally Harmonized System of Classification and Labelling of Chemicals")  | **Danger**  |  
| [Hazard statements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GHS_hazard_statements "GHS hazard statements")  | H228  |  
| [Precautionary statements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GHS_precautionary_statements "GHS precautionary statements")  |  P210, P240, P241, P280, P370+P378[[159]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-164)  |  
|  [**NFPA 704**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NFPA_704 "NFPA 704") (firediamond)  |  [[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-165) ![NFPA 704 four-colored diamond](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/NFPA_704.svg/120px-NFPA_704.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) [0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NFPA_704#Blue "NFPA 704") [4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NFPA_704#Red "NFPA 704") [1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NFPA_704#Yellow "NFPA 704")  |  
Chemical compound
Titanium is non-toxic, even in large doses, and does not play any natural role inside the [human body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_body "Human body").[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p451-36) An estimated 0.8milligrams of titanium is ingested by humans each day, but most passes through the [digestive system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digestive_system "Digestive system") without being absorbed in the tissues.[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p451-36) However, it can sometimes [bioaccumulate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioaccumulation "Bioaccumulation") in tissues that contain [silica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silica "Silica"). [Yellow nail syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_nail_syndrome "Yellow nail syndrome") has been reported in individuals that have been exposed to titanium, though the disorder's rarity have made it difficult to determine a direct association between exposure and disorder development.[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-166)[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-167)
As a powder or in the form of metal shavings, titanium metal poses a significant fire hazard and, when heated in [air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air "Air"), an explosion hazard.[[163]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-168) Water and [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide "Carbon dioxide") are ineffective for extinguishing a titanium fire; [Class D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_classes "Fire classes") dry powder agents must be used instead.[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-HistoryAndUse-15) When used in the production or handling of chlorine, titanium exposed to dry chlorine gas may result in a titanium–chlorine fire.[[164]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-169) Titanium can also catch fire when a fresh, non-oxidized surface comes in contact with [liquid oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_oxygen "Liquid oxygen").[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-170)
## Function in plants
[![The dark green dentated elliptic leaves of a nettle](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Kopiva.JPG/250px-Kopiva.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kopiva.JPG)[Nettles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urtica_dioica "Urtica dioica") contain up to 80 parts per million of titanium.[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p451-36)
An unknown mechanism in [plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant "Plant") may use titanium to stimulate the production of [carbohydrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbohydrate "Carbohydrate") and encourage growth. This may explain why most plants contain about 1 [part per million](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Part_per_million "Part per million") (ppm) of titanium, food plants have about 2ppm, and [horsetail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horsetail "Horsetail") and [nettle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urtica "Urtica") contain up to 80ppm.[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium#cite_note-Emsley2001p451-36)
